 Did you know?
The match for 3rd place in boxing was eliminated for the 1952 Olympics. Originally, this also meant the elimination of bronze medals in the sport, but this decision was later reversed, and the losing semi-finalists finally received their medals in 1970.

 Did you know?
Modern pentathlon débuted at the 1912 Olympics. Two people claim to have invented the sport: Olympic renovator Pierre de Coubertin and Gustaf Balck, one of the organizers of the 1912 Games.

 Did you know?
The American city of Denver, Colorado was elected to host the 1976 Winter Olympics, but in a referendum the population decided against them. The Games were eventually held in Innsbruck, Austria, which had also hosted the 1964 edition.

 Did you know?
Speed skating was conducted outdoors at the Winter Olympics until the advent of indoor ovals in the 1980s. Since 1994, an indoor oval is a requirement, and the last time speed skating was held outdoors was in Albertville, 1992.

 Did you know?
The 1948 Olympic ice hockey tournament saw two US entries: one affiliatied with the international Ice Hockey Federation, and one affiliated with the US Olympic Committee. Both were barred from entering, but the former competed anyway, only to be disqualified later.

 Did you know?
Handball was first held at the 1936 Olympics in Berlin as an outdoor sport. It did not return to the Games until the next time Germany hosted the Olympics: in 1972, it was an indoor sport.

 Did you know?
The United States has sent athletes to all Olympics since 1896 (both Summer and Winter Games), with the sole exception of the 1980 Summer Olympics. The US boycotted these over the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan.

 Did you know?
The first person to light the Olympic Flame and go on to win a gold medal in the same Olympics is Australia’s Cathy Freeman, who did so in 2000.

 Did you know?
Speed skater Guido Caroli carried the Olympic Torch on the ice during the opening ceremony of the 1956 Winter Olympics. He tripped over a microphone wire and fell, but fortunately he was not harmed and the flame did not go out.

 Did you know?
The 1948 Olympic ice hockey tournament saw two US entries: one affiliatied with the international Ice Hockey Federation, and one affiliated with the US Olympic Committee. Both were barred from entering, but the former competed anyway, only to be disqualified later.

 Did you know?
The only time in history that no bobsleigh events were held was in 1960. The organizers of the Games in Squaw Valley (now Paradise Tahoe), California, thought construction of a run was too expensive.

 Did you know?
Hungary competed in every Winter Olympics from 1924 on, but had to wait for 94 years to win their first Winter Olympics Gold medal in 2018.

 Did you know?
Norwegian figure skating star Sonia Henie won her third consecutive figure skating gold at the 1936 Winter Olympics. She placed last at her first Olympic appearance in 1924, when she was just 11 years old.

 Did you know?
The Vancouver Olympics in 2010 were the third Games held in Canada, but during the two previous editions, no Canadian athlete had won a gold medal. In Vancouver, they won 14.

 Did you know?
The Tikal brothers Steve Tikal (for Australia) and František Tikal (for Czechoslovakia) faced each other in an ice hockey match at the 1960 Winter Olympics to become the first brothers competing head-to-head for different nations.

 Did you know?
The high altitude of Mexico City (2,134 m above sea level) helped cause a barrage of world records in track and field events, notably Bob Beamon’s stunning 8.90 m long jump.

 Did you know?
The first Olympian to die was Rene Defert, a French tennis player who passed away four months after the end of the 1896 Games due to accidental poisoning.
